Split sessions into 60-second trials.\n    33→\n    34→### Decoder Inputs:\n    35→- Time elapsed from the beginning of the session in seconds. Time-varying.\n    36→\n    37→### Decoder Outputs\n    38→- Motion energy, discretized into five equal-percentile bins, selected per session. Time-varying.\n    39→\n    40→## Target Data Format\n    41→\n    42→- Save the full converted dataset to the pickle file `/app/converted_data.pkl`.\n    43→- All datasets must be converted into the following Python dictionary structure:\n    44→\n    45→```python\n    46→data = {\n    47→    'neural': [  # list of sessions\n    48→        [  # list of trials for session 1\n    49→            neuron_by_time_matrix,  # shape: (n_neurons, n_timepoints)\n    50→            neuron_by_time_matrix,  # trial 2\n    51→            ...\n    52→        ],\n    53→        [  # list of trials for session 2\n    54→            ...\n    55→        ],\n    56→        ...\n    57→    ],\n    58→\n    59→    'input': [  # list of sessions\n    60→        [  # list of trials for session 1\n    61→            input_data,  # stimulus/task variables, shape: (n_input, n_timepoints) or (n_input)\n    62→            ...\n    63→        ],\n    64→        ...\n    65→    ],\n    66→\n    67→    'output': [  # list of sessions\n    68→        [  # list of trials for session 1\n    69→            output_data,  # behavioral response, shape (n_output, n_timepoints) or (n_output)\n    70→            ...\n    71→        ],\n    72→        ...\n    73→    ],\n    74→\n    75→    'subjects': list of str, # ids of all subjects, length n_subjects\n    76→    'subject_idx': shape (n_sessions,), # index into subjects for each session\n    77→\n    78→    'brain_regions': list of str, # names of the brain regions recorded from, e.g. ALM, V1\n    79→    'brain_region_idx': [ # list of sessions\n    80→        neuron_region_idx, # shape: (n_neurons,)\n    81→    ], # index into `brain_regions` for each neuron\n    82→\n    83→    'input_names': list of str, # names for each input, of length n_input\n    84→    'output_names': list of str, # names of each output, of length n_output\n    85→    'output_values': [ # list of length n_output\n    86→        [ output_val0, ... ], # list of str of length d_output[0], name of each output value\n    87→        ...\n    88→    ],\n    89→\n    90→    'metadata': {\n    91→        'task_description': str, # description of output tasks\n    92→        'time_bin_size': float, # length of time bin in ms\n    93→        'temporal_alignment_event': str, # description of alignment event\n    94→        'off_start': float or None, # signed time from alignment event to start of trial in seconds, None if N/A\n    95→        'off_end': float or None, # signed time from alignment event to end of trial in seconds, None if N/A\n    96→        ...\n    97→    }\n    98→}\n    99→```\n   100→\n   101→### Key Format Requirements\n   102→\n   103→- **neural**: Neural activity data (e.g., firing rates, spike counts) organized by session and trial\n   104→  - Dimensions: (n_neurons, n_timepoints) for each trial.\n   105→  - Time bins should be the same size for all trials and sessions. \n   106→  - There needs to be at least two trials within each session in order to evaluate the decoder performance.\n   107→\n   108→- **input**: Variables that serve as inputs **to the decoder**\n   109→  - These are **decoder inputs**. They could be task inputs to the animal, but inputs to the animal can also be decoder outputs.\n   110→  - Examples: time from an aligning signal, stimulus characteristics, our animal behavior such as:\n   111→    - visual: contrast, position, orientation\n   112→    - audio: frequency, amplitude\n   113→    - behavior: running speed, pupil size\n   114→  - Dimensions: (d_input, n_timepoints) for each trial. \n   115→  - Should capture all relevant contextual information for the decoder\n   116→  - If an input is a time such as onset of some stimulus, represent it as a binary time series. \n   117→\n   118→- **output**: Variables to be decoded/predicted from neural activity\n   119→  - These are what we want the decoder to **predict**. They could be the animal's output behavior or properties of the stimulus.\n   120→  - Output variables must be **categorical**, i.e. discrete rather than continuous. If a variable is not discrete, follow instructions from Decoder Task to discretize it. \n   121→  - Examples: stimulus properties (contrast, orientation), choice (left/right), reaction time, position\n   122→  - Can be time-varying or discrete values per trial. If at all possible, make it time-varying\n   123→  - If an output is a time such as when a behavior occurred, represent it as a binary time series. \n   124→\n   125→- **subjects**: List of names of all unique subjects (mice).\n   126→- **subject_idx**: Index into subjects for each session.\n   127→  - Order should match order of sessions in `neural`, etc.\n   128→\n   129→- **brain_regions**: List of names of all brain regions recorded from.\n   130→- **brain_region_idx**: Index into brain_regions for each neuron in each session.\n   131→\n   132→- **input_names**: Names of each input variable.\n   133→  - Order should match order of `input` dimensions. \n   134→\n   135→- **output_names**: Names of each output variable.\n   136→  - Order should match order of `output` dimensions.\n   137→- **output_values**: Names of each output value for each output variable.\n   138→  - Order should match categorical `output` values.\n   139→\n   140→- **metadata**: Descriptive information. \n   141→  - `task_description`: Concise description of the task\n   142→  - `temporal_alignment_event`: Concise description of temporal alignment event\n   143→  - `off_start`: Signed time from alignment event to start of trial. Negative means before event, positive after. \n   144→  - `off_end`: Signed time from alignment event to end of trial\n   145→  - Add other relevant fields, e.g. `session_info`\n   146→\n   147→## Python environment\n   148→\n   149→- All required Python packages are already installed in the system Python environment.\n   150→- Use `python3` to run any python code.\n   151→- If you need to install additional libraries, use `pip install`.\n   152→\n   153→## Decoder Reference\n   154→\n   155→- Run this script with `python /app/train_decoder.py <data_file_path>` to validate.\n   156→- Optional arguments:\n   157→  - `--verify-only`: Only verify data format and print summary, then exit (does not train decoder).\n   158→  - `--plot-samples`: Plot sample trials from data and save to png files.\n   159→  - `--cpu`: Force torch to use CPU only (use if GPU runs out of memory).\n   160→- Pipe the output to the designated file so that the user can examine it.\n   161→\n   162→### Purpose\n   163→\n   164→These functions will be used during the validation phase to ensure:\n   165→1. Data structure is correct - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n   166→2. Dimensions are consistent - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n   167→3. Values are sensible - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n   168→4. Decoder can successfully train and predict\n   169→5. Poor performance indicates data formatting issues that need investigation\n   170→\n   171→### Consistency requirements\n   172→\n   173→Your processing and formatting must **match the reference paper and code** with respect to:\n   174→- Loading of data\n   175→- Temporal alignment of different time series streams (neural, inputs, outputs)\n   176→- Processing of neural, input, and output data streams\n   177→- Curation of data: filtering of low-quality neurons, trials, sessions, and mice.\n   178→\n   179→To check consistency, you must compare statistics available in the reference paper and your converted dataset. \n   180→\n   181→Whenever possible, invent **SANITY CHECKS** that your loading and processing matches the reference paper and code. \n   182→\n   183→Discrepancies are only allowed if required by the Decoder Input and Decoder Output specifications above, or the task of training a neural decoder.\n   184→\n   185→Carefully verify your work after every step. When you find mistakes, document and fix them immediately. Be critical of results **after every step of processing**. \n   186→\n   187→You will be assessed on whether the decisions you make on how to load, filter, process, align, reformat, and save the data are reasonable and match the reference paper and code and these instructions. During testing, the reference version of `/app/train_decoder.py` will be run on your converted data. Data statistics and decoder accuracy will be compared to those achieved by human-written conversion code. \n   188→\n   189→---\n   190→\n   191→## Conversion Workflow\n   192→\n   193→**IMPORTANT**: Follow these steps in order. Each step has explicit completion criteria. Do not proceed to the next step until the current step is complete. Use the exact step names below when documenting progress in CONVERSION_NOTES.md.\n   194→\n   195→**STATUS TRACKING**: At the start of each step, update its status to `IN PROGRESS` in CONVERSION_NOTES.md. When complete, update to `COMPLETE`. This helps you and the user track progress.\n   196→\n   197→---\n   198→\n   199→### Step 0: Setup and Initialization\n   200→\n   201→**Goal**: Set up the working environment and create the notes file.\n   202→\n   203→**Actions**:\n   204→1. **FIRST**: Create `/app/CONVERSION_NOTES.md` with the template structure shown at the end of this document\n   205→2. Verify you can run `python3` and import key packages (numpy, torch)\n   206→3. List the contents of this directory to understand what files are available\n   207→\n   208→**Done when**: \n   209→- `/app/CONVERSION_NOTES.md` exists with the proper template structure\n   210→- You have confirmed Python and key packages work\n   211→- You have listed directory contents in CONVERSION_NOTES.md\n   212→\n   213→**⚠️ CHECKPOINT**: Before proceeding to Step 1, verify that `/app/CONVERSION_NOTES.md` exists by running `ls -la /app/CONVERSION_NOTES.md`. If it doesn't exist, STOP and create it now.\n   214→\n   215→---\n   216→\n   217→### Step 1: Reference Code Exploration\n   218→\n   219→**⚠️ CONSTRAINT REMINDER**: Only look at code in the `/app/code` directory. Do NOT look outside this project directory.\n   220→\n   221→**Goal**: Understand how the original authors loaded and processed their data.\n   222→\n   223→**Actions**:\n   224→- Look for documentation or README files in the `/app/code` directory\n   225→- Read the provided reference code bases to see how the reference paper loaded and manipulated the data\n   226→- Understand which functions are being called to read, curate, process, and manipulate the data\n   227→- For neural imaging data, does delta F over F need to be computed? \n   228→- For electrophysiology neural data, do cells need to be filtered based on quality? \n   229→- Note to file important functions, their inputs, and outputs\n   230→\n   231→**Done when**: You have documented key functions and their purposes in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 1\".\n   232→\n   233→**⚠️ CHECKPOINT**: Before proceeding to Step 2, update Step 1 CONVERSION_NOTES.md.\n   234→\n   235→---\n   236→\n   237→### Step 2: Dataset Exploration\n   238→\n   239→**⚠️ CONSTRAINT REMINDER**: Only look at data in the `/app/data` directory. `--full` (default): Process all sessions\n   341→  - `--sample`: Process only 2 sessions for testing\n   342→  - `--show-processing`: Plot visualizations of EVERY processing step for up to 2 sessions. Save plots as `processing_<session_id>.png`.\n   343→- Reuse code from reference code bases where appropriate\n   344→- Write efficient code:\n   345→  - Vectorize loops\n   346→  - Avoid unnecessary file I/O\n   347→  - Use parallel processing if beneficial\n   348→  - Print timing information to find bottlenecks\n   349→- Plots for `--show-processing` mode should visually convince the user that every step of the conversion is correct.\n   350→  - There are no temporal misalignments\n   351→  - Discretization of continuous outputs is correct\n   352→- Write clear, well-documented functions\n   353→- Handle missing data appropriately (consult references, use sensible defaults, document)\n   354→- Validate data shapes and types at each step\n   355→- Include sanity checks (e.g., trial counts match across arrays)\n   356→\n   357→**Done when**: `/app/convert_data.py` exists and runs efficiently without errors.\n   358→\n   359→**⚠️ DO NOT PROCEED** to Step 7 until `/app/convert_data.py` exists and you have updated Step 6 in CONVERSION_NOTES.md.\n   360→\n   361→---\n   362→\n   363→### Step 7: Sample Conversion and Validation\n   364→\n   365→**Goal**: Validate the conversion on a small sample before processing the full dataset.\n   366→\n   367→**Actions**:\n   368→1. Run `python -u /app/convert_data.py /app/sample_data.pkl --sample --show-processing 2>&1 | tee /app/conversion_sample_out.txt`\n   369→  a. Verify the sample data structure matches the specification.\n   370→  b. Check dimensions are consistent across trials/sessions.\n   371→  c. Check that no data is missed during conversion. \n   372→  d. Validate that metadata accurately describes the data.\n   373→2. Improve efficiency of conversion.\n   374→  a. Look at and note timing information for the conversion. \n   375→  b. Estimate how long the full conversion will run. When you do this, make sure that your estimate accounts for differences in lengths/numbers of trials/sessions. \n   376→  c. If full conversion time estimate is longer than 15 minutes, speed up the code by writing more efficient code (vectorizing loops, removing unnecessary or redundant computations) and/or including parallel processing. \n   377→3. Run `python -u /app/train_decoder.py /app/sample_data.pkl --verify-only 2>&1 | tee /app/verification_sample_out.txt`.\n   378→4. Analyze the output file `/app/verification_sample_out.txt`:\n   379→  a. Verify no errors reported.\n   380→  b. Attempt to address any warnings\n   381→  c. Check input ranges and output value distributions against expectations from reference texts.\n   382→\n   383→**Done when**: \n   384→- `/app/sample_data.pkl` is created and passes manual inspection\n   385→- `/app/verification_sample_out.txt` is created and passes inspection\n   386→- Processing plots show no anomalies\n   387→- Document sample statistics in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 7\"\n   388→\n   389→**⚠️ DO NOT PROCEED** to Step 8 until `/app/sample_data.pkl` and `/app/verification_sample_out.txt` files exist, and CONVERSION_NOTES.md Step 7 is filled in. \n   390→\n   391→---\n   392→\n   393→### Step 8: Sample Decoder Training\n   394→\n   395→**Goal**: Verify the decoder runs successfully on sample data.\n   396→\n   397→**Actions**:\n   398→1. Review your time estimate from Step 7.\n   421→2. If estimated time is long (e.g. longer than 15 minutes), optimize bottlenecks first.\n   422→3. Run `python -u /app/convert_data.py /app/converted_data.pkl --full 2>&1 | tee /app/conversion_full_out.txt`\n   423→4. As the code runs, update your estimates of how long processing will take. If it is much longer than your previous estimate (> 1.5x), kill the process, optimize bottlenecks, and repeat. \n   424→5. Run `python -u /app/train_decoder.py /app/converted_data.pkl --verify-only 2>&1 | tee /app/verification_full_out.txt`. \n   425→6. Check that no data was lost during conversion \n   426→7. Spot-check a few sessions to verify data integrity\n   427→8. **Check for consistency** between dataset statistics in `/app/verification_full_out.txt` and the reference texts\n   428→9. Investigate any inconsistencies, and revise the conversion script until all dataset statistics are consistent\n   429→\n   430→**Done when**:\n   431→- `/app/converted_data.pkl` is created and passes manual inspection\n   432→- `/app/verification_full_out.txt` is created and passes inspection\n   433→- **ALL** dataset statistics are consistent with values from reference texts\n   434→- You have documented statistics and consistency in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 9\"\n   435→\n   436→**⚠️ DO NOT PROCEED** to Step 10 until `/app/converted_data.pkl` and `/app/verification_full_out.txt` files exist and CONVERSION_NOTES.MD Step 9 is filled in.\n   437→\n   438→---\n   439→\n   440→### Step 10: Critical Review 1\n   441→\n   442→**Goal**: Find and fix any errors by performing the following checks. Fix issues and re-run affected steps. Iterate until no issues remain.\n   443→\n   444→**Check 1: Output log verification**\n   445→Read through `/app/verification_full_out.txt` to find and fix **all errors**. Address each warning by either fixing its cause or verifying it that it is not possible to fix it. Explain why each warning could not be fixed in `/app/CONVERSION_NOTES.md`. \n   446→\n   447→**Check 2: Construct sanity checks**\n   448→- Develop at least one sanity check each on neural, input, and output data.\n   449→- These must involve loading in the original data files (NOT through your conversion code).\n   450→- Construct comparison tests and criteria using `np.allclose()`. \n   451→- These tests can be spot-checks of the data, e.g. neural activity at trial 5, timepoint 10, neuron 3. \n   452→- Fix all mismatches/sanity check failures. \n   453→- Document your sanity checks to `/app/CONVERSION_NOTES.md`. \n   454→\n   455→**Check 3: Reference code comparison**\n   456→- Compare your code to the reference code and methods. \n   457→- For each major processing step — (a) data loading, (b) neuron/trial filtering, (c) temporal alignment, (d) binning, (e) input construction, (f) output construction — identify the corresponding code in your script and in the reference, and compare the logic. \n   458→- Check whether you use the same variables, filtering, and processing as the reference code. \n   459→- Fix any mismatches. \n   460→- Document your comparisons in `/app/CONVERSION_NOTES.md`. \n   461→- Explain your reasoning for any differences in `/app/CONVERSION_NOTES.md`.\n   462→\n   463→**Check 4: Key statistics comparison**\n   464→- Verify that key statistics (number of animals, sessions, trials, neurons, input and output ranges, output distributions) match information available in the reference paper. \n   465→- Write code to investigate any discrepancies. \n   466→- Fix any discovered issues. \n   467→- Document your comparisons in `/app/CONVERSION_NOTES.md`. \n   468→\n   469→**Check 5: Check for edge cases**\n   470→- Check for off-by-one errors at the beginning/ends of trials, sessions, and subjects, or in definitions of the inputs and outputs. \n   471→- There may be minor issues in the data that your code must handle. Check that your code robustly handles issues in the original data. \n   472→\n   473→**Iteration protocol**: If ANY check above reveals an issue:\n   474→1. Fix the issue in convert_data.py\n   475→2. Re-run the affected conversion and validation steps\n   476→3. Re-run ALL checks in this step (not just the one that failed)\n   477→4. Document each iteration: what was found, what was fixed, what the re-check showed\n   478→\n   479→Write a report to CONVERSION_NOTES.md describing **every** check you did, to help convince the user that the conversion code works. **Done when**: You have documented your review findings and all issues are resolved in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 10\".\n   480→\n   481→**⚠️ DO NOT PROCEED** to Step 11 until you have checked for consistency between **every** statistic in the reference texts, code, and data and documented in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under Step 10. \n   482→\n   483→---\n   484→\n   485→### Step 11: Full Decoder Training\n   486→\n   487→**Goal**: Validate the decoder on the **complete** dataset.\n   488→\n   489→**⚠️ IMPORTANT**: This step must complete fully. Do not skip or abbreviate. Do not proceed to Step 12 until this step is completely finished.\n   490→\n   491→**Actions**:\n   492→1. Run `python -u /app/train_decoder.py /app/converted_data.pkl --plot-samples 2>&1 | tee /app/train_decoder_full_out.txt`. If the GPU does not have sufficient RAM for the network training, use the flag `--cpu` to specify to use the CPU to train. \n   493→2. **Wait for complete execution** — this may take significant time for large datasets\n   494→3. **Check training**: Verify loss decreases over epochs\n   495→4. **Check accuracy**: High accuracy is an indicator that data conversion has been done correctly. Accuracy near chance is a sign that there might be issues in temporal alignment of signals, choice of data streams, or filtering or processing of data. Compare accuracy to expectations based on the paper. \n   496→\n   497→**Done when**: \n   498→1. Full decoder training completes (the script finishes running)\n   499→2. Accuracy results are documented in CONVERSION_NOTES.md under \"Step 11\" with a table of accuracies\n   500→\n   501→---\n   502→\n   503→### Step 12: Critical Review 2\n   504→\n   505→**Goal**: Find and fix any errors by performing the following checks. Fix issues and re-run affected steps. Iterate until no issues remain. Perform all of the following checks:\n   506→\n   507→**Check 1: Accuracy vs chance analysis**\n   508→- For each output, compare validation accuracy to chance (1/num_classes)\n   509→- Accuracy below chance indicates a bug, investigate and fix conversion issues to improve this. \n   510→- Accuracy below 1.5x chance may also indicate a bug, investigate whether changes in conversion logic could improve accuracy. \n   511→\n   512→**Check 2: Accuracy comparison to paper**\n   513→- Find every decoding accuracy reported in the reference paper\n   514→- Create a table comparing your accuracy to the paper's accuracy for each variable\n   515→- If your accuracy is lower than the reported accuracy, this may indicate a bug in your conversion that you must fix. \n   516→- Differences in algorithm/architecture should be relatively small because of the data dimensionality and size. Do this BEFORE exploring any code or data. Document everything in this file as you work.\n\n2. **FOLLOW STEPS IN ORDER**: Do not skip steps. Do not proceed to step N+1 until step N is complete. Each step has \"Done when\" criteria — verify them before moving on.\n\n3. **MATCH THE REFERENCE PROCESSING WHEN APPLICABLE**: Your processing must match what's described in the reference paper and code, except where the decoder input and output specifications or training a neural decoder require otherwise. You will be assessed on the statistics and decoder accuracy of your converted data, and on whether an expert would consider each of your decisions well justified.\n\n---\n\n## Project Context\n\n- You are a computational neuroscientist. Your goal is to load and reformat data from a neuroscience paper into a specified structure suitable for downstream analysis.\n- You need to use the **SAME** processing of the data described in the provided reference paper and code repository when applicable. This requires examining the provided paper, code, and data to understand how the data is formatted and processed, and determine how to balance this with the requirements of the new downstream analysis.\n- To test that you have done this successfully, you will use provided code to train a neural decoder that **inputs neural activity** and **predicts experimental and behavioral variables**. \n- We will assess your work in two ways. First, statistics of the converted data and decoder accuracy will be compared to those of an expert-written solution. Second, each decision you make about how to load, filter, process, align, and save the data will be compared with the decision made by an expert. A decision that differs from the expert's is acceptable if it is equally well justified; decisions that are poorly justified will be flagged as concerning or incorrect.\n\n## Reference Information\n\n- The reference **paper** \"longitudinal tracking of neuronal activity from the same cells in the developing brain using Track2p\" is in the file `/app/paper.pdf`.\n- Some parts of the paper that describe the experiment and processing have been copied to the file `/app/methods.txt`.\n- **Code** from the paper are in the directory `/app/code`.\n- **Data** from the paper are in the directory `/app/data`.\n\n## Decoder Task\nDecode information regarding animal motion from the neural activities recorded from mouse barrel cortex. Split sessions into 60-second trials.\n\n### Decoder Inputs:\n- Time elapsed from the beginning of the session in seconds. Time-varying.\n\n### Decoder Outputs\n- Motion energy, discretized into five equal-percentile bins, selected per session. Time-varying.\n\n## Target Data Format\n\n- Save the full converted dataset to the pickle file `/app/converted_data.pkl`.\n- All datasets must be converted into the following Python dictionary structure:\n\n```python\ndata = {\n    'neural': [  # list of sessions\n        [  # list of trials for session 1\n            neuron_by_time_matrix,  # shape: (n_neurons, n_timepoints)\n            neuron_by_time_matrix,  # trial 2\n            ...\n        ],\n        [  # list of trials for session 2\n            ...\n        ],\n        ...\n    ],\n\n    'input': [  # list of sessions\n        [  # list of trials for session 1\n            input_data,  # stimulus/task variables, shape: (n_input, n_timepoints) or (n_input)\n            ...\n        ],\n        ...\n    ],\n\n    'output': [  # list of sessions\n        [  # list of trials for session 1\n            output_data,  # behavioral response, shape (n_output, n_timepoints) or (n_output)\n            ...\n        ],\n        ...\n    ],\n\n    'subjects': list of str, # ids of all subjects, length n_subjects\n    'subject_idx': shape (n_sessions,), # index into subjects for each session\n\n    'brain_regions': list of str, # names of the brain regions recorded from, e.g. ALM, V1\n    'brain_region_idx': [ # list of sessions\n        neuron_region_idx, # shape: (n_neurons,)\n    ], # index into `brain_regions` for each neuron\n\n    'input_names': list of str, # names for each input, of length n_input\n    'output_names': list of str, # names of each output, of length n_output\n    'output_values': [ # list of length n_output\n        [ output_val0, ... ], # list of str of length d_output[0], name of each output value\n        ...\n    ],\n\n    'metadata': {\n        'task_description': str, # description of output tasks\n        'time_bin_size': float, # length of time bin in ms\n        'temporal_alignment_event': str, # description of alignment event\n        'off_start': float or None, # signed time from alignment event to start of trial in seconds, None if N/A\n        'off_end': float or None, # signed time from alignment event to end of trial in seconds, None if N/A\n        ...\n    }\n}\n```\n\n### Key Format Requirements\n\n- **neural**: Neural activity data (e.g., firing rates, spike counts) organized by session and trial\n  - Dimensions: (n_neurons, n_timepoints) for each trial.\n  - Time bins should be the same size for all trials and sessions. \n  - There needs to be at least two trials within each session in order to evaluate the decoder performance.\n\n- **input**: Variables that serve as inputs **to the decoder**\n  - These are **decoder inputs**. They could be task inputs to the animal, but inputs to the animal can also be decoder outputs.\n  - Examples: time from an aligning signal, stimulus characteristics, our animal behavior such as:\n    - visual: contrast, position, orientation\n    - audio: frequency, amplitude\n    - behavior: running speed, pupil size\n  - Dimensions: (d_input, n_timepoints) for each trial. \n  - Should capture all relevant contextual information for the decoder\n  - If an input is a time such as onset of some stimulus, represent it as a binary time series. \n\n- **output**: Variables to be decoded/predicted from neural activity\n  - These are what we want the decoder to **predict**. They could be the animal's output behavior or properties of the stimulus.\n  - Output variables must be **categorical**, i.e. discrete rather than continuous. If a variable is not discrete, follow instructions from Decoder Task to discretize it. \n  - Examples: stimulus properties (contrast, orientation), choice (left/right), reaction time, position\n  - Can be time-varying or discrete values per trial. If at all possible, make it time-varying\n  - If an output is a time such as when a behavior occurred, represent it as a binary time series. \n\n- **subjects**: List of names of all unique subjects (mice).\n- **subject_idx**: Index into subjects for each session.\n  - Order should match order of sessions in `neural`, etc.\n\n- **brain_regions**: List of names of all brain regions recorded from.\n- **brain_region_idx**: Index into brain_regions for each neuron in each session.\n\n- **input_names**: Names of each input variable.\n  - Order should match order of `input` dimensions. \n\n- **output_names**: Names of each output variable.\n  - Order should match order of `output` dimensions.\n- **output_values**: Names of each output value for each output variable.\n  - Order should match categorical `output` values.\n\n- **metadata**: Descriptive information. \n  - `task_description`: Concise description of the task\n  - `temporal_alignment_event`: Concise description of temporal alignment event\n  - `off_start`: Signed time from alignment event to start of trial. Negative means before event, positive after. \n  - `off_end`: Signed time from alignment event to end of trial\n  - Add other relevant fields, e.g. `session_info`\n\n## Python environment\n\n- All required Python packages are already installed in the system Python environment.\n- Use `python3` to run any python code.\n- If you need to install additional libraries, use `pip install`.\n\n## Decoder Reference\n\n- Run this script with `python /app/train_decoder.py <data_file_path>` to validate.\n- Optional arguments:\n  - `--verify-only`: Only verify data format and print summary, then exit (does not train decoder).\n  - `--plot-samples`: Plot sample trials from data and save to png files.\n  - `--cpu`: Force torch to use CPU only (use if GPU runs out of memory).\n- Pipe the output to the designated file so that the user can examine it.\n\n### Purpose\n\nThese functions will be used during the validation phase to ensure:\n1. Data structure is correct - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n2. Dimensions are consistent - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n3. Values are sensible - will report errors and warnings to stdout. \n4. Decoder can successfully train and predict\n5. Do not use this as an excuse to dismiss differences, investigate them completely before moving on. \n\n**Check 3: Train vs validation gap**\n- If training accuracy is much higher (>1.5x) than validation accuracy for any output, investigate overfitting or data leakage. \n\n**If accuracy is low for an output, try these specific debugging steps:**\n1. Verify the output values are correct by loading raw data and checking 3 specific trials\n2. Check temporal alignment — plot neural + output for a single trial to verify they're synchronized\n3. 1:\n        trimmed = values[:usable]\n        return trimmed.reshape(-1, bin_frames).mean(axis=1).astype(np.float32, copy=False)\n    if values.ndim == 2:\n        trimmed = values[:, :usable]\n discretize_motion_quintiles(motion_binned: np.ndarray) -> tuple[np.ndarray, np.ndarray, str]:\n    edges = np.quantile(motion_binned, [0.2, 0.4, 0.6, 0.8]).astype(np.float32)\n    discrete = np.searchsorted(edges, motion_binned, side=\"right\").astype(np.int64)\n    if np.unique(discrete).size < 5:\n        order = np.argsort(motion_binned, kind=\"stable\")\n        discrete = np.empty_like(order, dtype=np.int64)\n        discrete[order] = np.minimum(4, (5 * np.arange(motion_binned.size)) // motion_binned.size)\n        method = \"rank_fallback\"\n    else:\n        method tuple[list[np.ndarray], list[np.ndarray], list[np.ndarray]]:\n    n_bins = neural_binned.shape[1]\n    usable = (n_bins // TRIAL_BINS) * TRIAL_BINS\n    if usable < TRIAL_BINS * 2:\n        raise * TRIAL_BINS, (trial_idx + 1) * TRIAL_BINS)\n        neural_trials.append(neural_binned[:, sl].astype(np.float32, copy=False))\n        input_trials.append(time_binned[np.newaxis, sl].astype(np.float32, copy=False))\n        output_trials.append(motion_bins[np.newaxis, sl].astype(np.int64, copy=False))\n\n    recon_neural = np.concatenate(neural_trials, axis=1)\n    recon_time = np.concatenate([trial[0] for trial in input_trials])\n    recon_output = np.concatenate([trial[0] for trial in output_trials])\n    if not np.allclose(recon_neural, neural_binned):\n        raise ValueError(\"Neural trial splitting failed reconstruction check.\")\n    if not np.allclose(recon_time, time_binned):\n        raise ValueError(\"Input trial splitting failed reconstruction check.\")\n    if not np.array_equal(recon_output, motion_bins):\n        raise ValueError(\"Output trial splitting failed reconstruction check.\")\n\n    return neural_trials, input_trials, does not implement behavioral decoding. It implements longitudinal cell registration/tracking and exports matched neural traces in suite2p-compatible format.\n    52→- The key curation rule in the reference code is ROI filtering by suite2p `iscell`. If `track_ops.iscell_thr` is `None`, keep rows with `iscell[:, 0] == 1`; otherwise keep rows with `iscell[:, 1] > iscell_thr`. The default threshold is `0.50`.\n    53→- The code preserves suite2p trace arrays directly (`F.npy`, `Fneu.npy`, `spks.npy`) after `iscell` filtering and cell matching. It does not compute `dF/F` anywhere in the repository sections inspected for loading/export, so conversion should not invent a `dF/F` transform unless raw data forces it.\n    54→- `save_in_s2p_format()` first removes cells not matched across all days in a match matrix row (`t2p_match_mat_allday = t2p_match_mat[~np.any(t2p_match_mat == None, axis=1), :]`) and then indexes filtered `F/Fneu/spks/stat/iscell` arrays with those matched-cell indices. This is the core longitudinal-neuron selection logic.\n    55→- Mean images and ROI shapes are used for registration/visualization only. For downstream decoding, the relevant reference outputs are the matched neural traces and the suite2p metadata (`ops`, `stat`, `iscell`, match matrices, suite2p indices).\n    56→- The code assumes imaging data in suite2p organization, supports multiple planes, and can save one matched suite2p folder per session/day under `track2p/matched_suite2p/`.\n    57→- No code related to motion-energy extraction, trialization, temporal binning for decoder inputs/outputs, or session splitting into 60 s trials appears in the reference repository. Those parts will need to come from the raw data organization and paper/methods, while respecting the above suite2p/Track2p cell filtering and matching decisions.\n    58→\n    59→---\n    60→\n    61→## Step 2: Dataset Exploration\n    62→**Status**: COMPLETE\n    63→\n    64→### Data Structure\n    65→- `/app/data/README.md` describes the release and folder organization.\n    66→- There are 6 subject folders: `jm031`, `jm032`, `jm038`, `jm039`, `jm040`, `jm046`.\n    67→- Each subject contains daily session folders named by recording date plus `_a` suffix.\n    68→- Each session contains:\n    69→  - `suite2p/plane0/`\n    70→    - `F.npy`: raw fluorescence traces, shape `(n_neurons, n_frames)`, `float32`\n    71→    - `Fneu.npy`: neuropil fluorescence traces\n    72→    - `spks.npy`: suite2p deconvolved activity, shape `(n_neurons, n_frames)`, `float32`\n    73→    - `iscell.npy`: shape `(n_neurons, 2)`; already matched/exported Track2p cells\n    74→    - `ops.npy`: suite2p metadata, includes `fs=30`, `nframes`, `meanImg`\n    75→    - `stat.npy`: suite2p ROI metadata per cell\n    76→  - `move_deve/`\n    77→    - `motion_energy_glob.npy`: processed global motion energy from behavior video, one value per available camera frame\n    78→    - `tstamps.npy`: timestamps for available behavior frames\n    79→    - `interframe_int.npy`: inter-frame intervals used to identify dropped/missing camera frames\n    80→- `load_data.ipynb` in `/app/data` provides example loading. It explicitly says `F.npy` contains raw fluorescence and suggests either computing `dF/F` “the way as described in the paper” or using `spks.npy` directly.\n    81→- `ground_truth.csv` exists for `jm038`, `jm039`, and `jm046`. It is a semicolon-separated table of manual cross-day cell correspondences (`D1`-`D7` columns). This looks useful for Track2p evaluation, not for the requested decoder.\n    82→- All inspected sessions have exactly one suite2p plane: `plane0`.\n    83→- The suite2p folders already contain neurons “present across all days” for that subject, so neuron row identities are aligned across days within each subject per `/app/data/README.md`.\n    84→\n    85→### Dataset Size (from data files)\n    86→| Statistic | Value |\n    87→|-----------|-------|\n    88→| Neurons (total) | 2,998 unique matched neurons across subjects; 20,445 neuron-session entries summed across all sessions |\n    89→| Neurons / session | Subject-specific constant counts: 221 (`jm031`), 370 (`jm032`), 685 (`jm038`), 746 (`jm039`), 541 (`jm040`), 435 (`jm046`) |\n    90→| Subjects | 6 |\n    91→| Sessions / subject | `jm031`: 7, `jm032`: 7, `jm038`: 7, `jm039`: 7, `jm040`: 6, `jm046`: 7 |\n    92→| Trials (total) | No native trials; continuous recordings. If split into 60 s windows at 30 Hz, there are 1,090 exact full windows total |\n    93→| Trials / session | No native trials; 20 windows for 36,000-frame sessions and 30 windows for 54,000-frame sessions |\n    94→\n    95→Additional native-data observations:\n    96→- 41 sessions total.\n    97→- Session frame counts are either 36,000 frames (20 min at 30 Hz) or 54,000 frames (30 min at 30 Hz).\n    98→- `ops['nframes']` matches `F.shape[1]` in inspected sessions.\n    99→- `motion_energy_glob.npy` and `tstamps.npy` always have the same length.\n   100→- In 32/41 sessions, motion length matches imaging frame count exactly.\n   101→- In 9/41 sessions, camera-frame samples are missing relative to imaging frames by 1, 2, 3, 116, or 148 frames depending on session.\n   102→- Missing behavior frames are visible as approximately doubled intervals in `interframe_int.npy`, consistent with `/app/data/README.md`.\n   103→- `tstamps.npy` appears to be in kiloseconds rather than seconds: `(session_duration_seconds) / (tstamps[-1] - tstamps[0])` is consistently about 992, so multiplying timestamps by 1000 should recover near-second units.\n   104→\n   105→---\n   106→\n   107→## Step 3: Reference Text Reading\n   108→**Status**: COMPLETE\n   109→\n   110→### Expected Statistics (from paper/methods)\n   111→| Statistic | Value | Source Quote |\n   112→|-----------|-------|--------------|\n   113→| Neurons (total) | Average 526 ± 190 tracked neurons per mouse across all days | “On average 526 (± 190 std) neurons per mouse were successfully tracked across all days using Track2p” |\n   114→| Neurons / session | Hundreds of tracked neurons per mouse/day; same tracked population across days within mouse | “yielded hundreds of identified neurons tracked across all days” |\n   115→| Subjects | 6 mice | “we used a full dataset of 6 mice imaged daily” |\n   116→| Sessions / subject | Minimum 6 consecutive daily sessions; example dataset 7 sessions from one mouse | “for at least 6 consecutive days” and “n=7 imaging sessions from one mouse” |\n   117→| Trials (total) | No native trials described; recordings are continuous sessions | Paper describes continuous recordings and 2 min blocks for cross-validation, not trialized data |\n   118→| Trials / session | Not applicable natively; session is continuous | “each session lasted 20 minutes” |\n   119→| Neural data time bin | Native sampling 30 Hz; decoding analyses average 10 timestamps | “Imaging rate was 30 Hz” and “averaging in bins of 10 consecutive timestamps” |\n   120→| Behavior data time bin | Native videography 30 Hz; decoding analyses average 10 timestamps | “Videos were recorded at 30 Hz” and “averaging in bins of 10 consecutive timestamps” |\n   121→| Reward rate | Not applicable | No reward-based task; spontaneous behavior dataset |\n   122→| Motion variable | Global motion energy from squared pixelwise frame differences | “computed their pixelwise difference… squared all individual pixel-wise values and summed across pixels” |\n   123→| Decoder evaluation metric | Continuous regression performance reported as R2, same-day and cross-day | “Graphs indicating R2 values for same-day cross-validated decoding performance” |\n   124→| Developmental transition | Behavioral-state representation emerges around P11 | “marking the emergence of a stable behavioral state representation” |\n   125→\n   126→\n   127→### Processing Details\n   128→- Imaging modality: chronic 2-photon calcium imaging in mouse barrel cortex layer 2/3, 512×512 pixels, 720×720 µm field of view.\n   129→- Sampling/alignment: imaging at 30 Hz; videography at 30 Hz with microscope acquisition triggering camera frames, described as allowing “simple synchronisation across the two modalities.”\n   130→- Neural preprocessing used in paper: Suite2p motion correction, ROI detection, signal extraction, spike deconvolution, then keep ROIs with Suite2p cell-classifier probability above 0.5.\n   131→- Neural signal used for downstream analyses in paper: baseline-corrected fluorescence traces treated as dF/F using default Suite2p parameters.\n   132→- Behavioral preprocessing: motion energy computed from consecutive video-frame differences, squaring pixel differences and summing over pixels to get one scalar per timepoint.\n   133→- Decoder preprocessing in paper: both dF/F and behavior traces are slightly denoised by averaging in bins of 10 consecutive timestamps (at 30 Hz this corresponds to ~333 ms bins).\n   134→- Same-day decoder evaluation in paper: nested cross-validation with inner and outer 5-fold loops, splitting recordings into consecutive 2-minute blocks.\n   135→- Cross-day decoder evaluation in paper: fit on one day, test on all other days using the tracked matched-neuron population.\n   136→\n   137→### Curation Steps\n   138→\n   139→**Neuron curation rules**:\n   140→- Use Suite2p preprocessing separately on each recording.\n   141→- Accept ROIs classified as true cells using the default Suite2p threshold of 0.5.\n   142→- Track cells across days with Track2p and analyze the neurons successfully tracked across all recorded days for a mouse.\n   143→\n   144→**Trial curation rules**:\n   145→- No native trials are described in the paper; recordings are continuous spontaneous-behavior sessions.\n   146→- Decoder evaluation blocks in the paper use consecutive 2-minute chunks for cross-validation, but the user task explicitly requires conversion into 60-second trials for downstream decoding.\n   147→\n   148→### Decoders Trained\n   149→| Decoded variable | Accuracy |\n   150→| Mouse motion / behavioral state from neural population activity | No explicit numeric table in text; figure captions/reporting use R2 and state that same-day decoding increases with development and late cross-day decoding is accurate/stable |\n   151→\n   152→---\n   153→\n   154→## Step 4: Check for Consistency\n   155→**Status**: COMPLETE\n   156→\n   157→### Discrepancies Found\n   158→| Topic | Code says | Data shows | Paper says | Resolution |\n   159→|-------|-----------|------------|------------|------------|\n   160→| Neural data representation | Track2p code exports matched `F`, `Fneu`, `spks`, `stat`, `iscell` in suite2p format; no `dF/F` computation inside repository | Release contains matched `F.npy`, `Fneu.npy`, `spks.npy`, `ops.npy`, `stat.npy`, `iscell.npy` per session | Downstream analyses used “baseline corrected fluorescence traces as our dF/F (using the default Suite2p parameters)” | Treat Track2p output as the matched-neuron substrate and reproduce Suite2p-style dF/F preprocessing from the saved suite2p arrays/ops when building decoder inputs. If exact reconstruction proves impossible, document the fallback. |\n   161→| Cell filtering | Reference code applies `iscell[:,1] > 0.5` by default | Released matched sessions already satisfy this: minimum observed `iscell[:,1]` across sessions is `0.500251773...` | Paper says “We considered all ROIs above the default threshold of 0.5 as true cells.” | No extra ROI filtering beyond the released matched suite2p files is needed; the data are already filtered consistently with code/paper. |\n   162→| Longitudinal matching | Track2p `save_in_s2p_format()` keeps only rows matched across all days for a mouse | Data README states neurons are “present across all days” and row identities match across days within mouse; neuron count is constant within a subject across sessions | Paper reports neurons “tracked across all days” for downstream analyses | Use the provided session files directly as already matched across all recorded days per subject; no need to rerun Track2p. |\n   163→| Session duration | Track2p code is agnostic to duration | Sessions are either 36,000 frames (20 min) or 54,000 frames (30 min) at 30 Hz | Methods text says “each session lasted 20 minutes” | Use actual frame counts in the released data as the source of truth and document the mismatch. This likely reflects heterogeneity between cohorts or an imprecise summary in the manuscript. |\n   164→| Behavior-neural alignment | Track2p code does not handle behavior | Motion arrays usually match imaging length, but 9 sessions have missing behavior frames detectable in `tstamps.npy`/`interframe_int.npy` | Data README explicitly mentions missing camera frames and says missing-frame indices can be obtained from timestamps / interframe intervals; methods say camera was triggered by microscope at 30 Hz | Reconstruct per-imaging-frame behavior by using timestamps/interframe intervals to place available motion samples on the full imaging frame grid, then interpolate or otherwise fill only the missing camera-frame positions. |\n   165→| Decoder protocol | Track2p repository has no decoder | Data are continuous recordings, not trials | Paper decoding uses ridge regression on continuous traces, 10-frame averaging, and 2-minute CV blocks | For the requested deliverable, preserve the paper’s 10-frame denoising/binning but adapt the representation to the user’s 60-second trial format and categorical output requirement. |\n   166→\n   167→---\n   168→\n   169→## Step 5: Mapping Planning\n   170→**Status**: COMPLETE\n   171→\n   172→### Variable Mapping\n   173→| Source Variable | Target Field | Transform | Reference Code Function(s) | Notes |\n   174→|-----------------|--------------|-----------|----------------------------|-------|\n   175→| `suite2p/plane0/F.npy` + `suite2p/plane0/Fneu.npy` + `suite2p/plane0/ops.npy` | `neural` | Compute `dF = F - ops['neucoeff'] * Fneu`, then apply Suite2p `dcnv.preprocess()` with saved default params (`baseline`, `win_baseline`, `sig_baseline`, `fs`, `prctile_baseline`, `batch_size`); average in non-overlapping 10-frame bins; split into 60 s trials; transpose to `(n_neurons, n_timepoints)` per trial | Track2p `save_in_s2p_format()` preserves matched `F/Fneu`; Suite2p `pipeline_s2p.py` deconvolution path shows `dF = F - neucoeff*Fneu` then `preprocess()` | Chosen to match the paper’s downstream analyses, which use Suite2p baseline-corrected fluorescence traces as dF/F. |\n   176→| Imaging frame index / `ops['fs']` | `input[0]` | Construct elapsed-time array in seconds on the imaging axis, then average in the same 10-frame bins and split into 60 s trials. Use absolute time from session start, not time-within-trial | Not from Track2p code; required by user decoder spec | One input dimension named `time_from_session_start_sec`. |\n   177→| `move_deve/motion_energy_glob.npy` + `tstamps.npy` + `interframe_int.npy` | `output[0]` | Reconstruct full per-imaging-frame motion series by assigning measured motion to frame indices inferred from timestamps and filling missing camera frames by interpolation; average in non-overlapping 10-frame bins; compute session-specific quintile edges from the full binned session; discretize into 5 categories; split into 60 s trials | No corresponding Track2p code; follows data README + paper motion-energy definition | One time-varying categorical output named `motion_energy_bin`; categories are session-specific quintiles. |\n   178→| Subject folder name (e.g. `jm031`) | `subjects`, `subject_idx` | Unique sorted subject list; per-session subject index | N/A | Session order will follow subject/date sorting for reproducibility. |\n   179→| Paper/methods metadata | `brain_regions`, `brain_region_idx` | Single region list `['barrel cortex L2/3']`; all neurons map to region index 0 | N/A | All recordings are from barrel cortex layer 2/3. |\n   180→\n   181→### Key Decisions\n   182→1. **Use matched Suite2p exports directly**: The release already contains Track2p outputs restricted to neurons present across all days within each mouse, which matches the paper’s downstream analysis population and avoids rerunning the tracking algorithm.\n   183→2. **Use Suite2p-style baseline-corrected fluorescence rather than raw `F.npy` or `spks.npy` for `neural`**: The paper explicitly states that subsequent analyses, including decoding, used baseline-corrected fluorescence traces as dF/F. Because `F.npy` is raw fluorescence and `Fneu.npy` plus `ops.npy` are available, reproducing the Suite2p preprocessing is better matched to the reference than using `spks.npy`.\n   184→3. **Apply the paper’s 10-frame averaging before decoder formatting**: This preserves the denoising/binning used in the reference decoding while reducing dimensionality. At 30 Hz, 10 frames = 1/3 s bins, so 60 s trials contain exactly 180 time bins.\n   185→4. **Use imaging frames as the canonical time axis**: Neural traces are complete on this axis; behavior is mapped onto it. This avoids drifting to the shorter camera series in sessions with dropped video frames.\n   186→5. **Interpolate only missing camera-frame positions**: The data README explicitly points to timestamps/inter-frame intervals for handling missing camera frames. Filling only inferred missing samples preserves measured motion everywhere else while producing aligned dense behavior for the decoder.\n   187→6. **Split sessions into contiguous 60 s windows without overlap**: This matches the user requirement and is exact because all sessions are multiples of 1,800 imaging frames (60 s at 30 Hz).\n   188→7. **Use session-specific motion-energy quintiles after denoising**: The user requires five equal-percentile bins “selected per session.” Applying percentiles on the binned behavior series aligns the labels with the actual decoder target time base.\n   189→8. **Keep all sessions and all full windows**: Every session yields at least 20 trials, so no session needs exclusion for insufficient trials. No extra neuron/session filtering beyond the provided matched-cell release is justified.\n   190→9. **Represent input as absolute session time in seconds**: The user asked specifically for time elapsed from session start. This variable should continue increasing across trials rather than resetting at each 60 s boundary.\n   191→\n   192→### Planned Sanity Checks\n   193→- [ ] Neural preprocessing sanity check: for selected neurons/session, verify converted neural bins equal manual computation from raw `F`, `Fneu`, `ops`, Suite2p `preprocess()`, and 10-frame averaging via `np.allclose()`.\n   194→- [ ] Behavior alignment sanity check: for a no-drop session, verify reconstructed full motion equals raw `motion_energy_glob.npy` exactly; for a drop session, verify inferred missing-frame count equals `n_imaging_frames - len(motion)` and non-missing positions match exactly.\n   195→- [ ] Trialization sanity check: verify each trial is exactly 180 bins and concatenating all trials reconstructs the full-session binned arrays.\n   196→- [ ] Output discretization sanity check: verify per-session class fractions are approximately 0.2 each after quintile binning, allowing small deviations from ties.\n   197→- [ ] Metadata/count sanity check: verify subject count, session count, neurons/session, and trials/session match the raw data and paper-derived expectations.\n   198→\n   199→---\n   200→\n   201→## Step 6: Script Development\n   202→**Status**: COMPLETE\n   203→\n   204→Implemented `/app/convert_data.py` with the following structure:\n   205→- Session discovery from `/app/data` with deterministic ordering.\n   206→- Representative `--sample` mode selecting two sessions that exercise both a dropped-camera-frames edge case and a different session duration.\n   207→- Suite2p-style neural preprocessing:\n   208→  - load `F.npy`, `Fneu.npy`, `ops.npy`\n   209→  - compute neuropil-subtracted traces `F - neucoeff * Fneu`\n   210→  - apply `suite2p.extraction.dcnv.preprocess()` using saved per-session Suite2p parameters\n   211→- Behavior alignment:\n   212→  - infer available behavior-frame positions from `tstamps.npy`\n   213→  - reconstruct a dense motion-energy series on the imaging frame grid\n   214→  - linearly interpolate only missing camera-frame positions\n   215→- Downstream formatting:\n   216→  - average neural, time, and motion traces in 10-frame bins\n   217→  - discretize binned motion into five session-specific bins\n   218→  - split each session into non-overlapping 60 s trials\n   219→  - package as the required dictionary and save to pickle\n   220→- Processing visualizations saved as `processing_<subject>_<session>.png` when `--show-processing` is used.\n   221→- Runtime reporting with per-session timing and ETA.\n   222→\n   223→Code inefficiencies identified:\n   224→- Plot generation reloads `ops.npy` once inside the plotting helper; negligible for current scale but avoidable if necessary.\n   225→- Conversion is currently single-process by session; likely acceptable for 41 sessions, but full-run timing will determine whether further optimization is required.\n   226→\n   227→Code speedups added:\n   228→- Use `mmap_mode='r'` when discovering sessions so cataloging does not load full arrays.\n   229→- Process one session at a time to cap peak memory.\n   230→- Use vectorized PROCESSING | Converts simplified NumPy input (`F.npy`, `fov.npy`, `rois.npy`) into suite2p-style folder structure. |\n| `DefaultTrackOps.__init__` | `code/track2p/ops/default.py` | CURATION | Defines default tracking parameters; critically sets `iscell_thr = 0.50`. |\n\n### Notes\n- The repository does not implement behavioral decoding. It implements longitudinal cell registration/tracking and exports matched neural traces in suite2p-compatible format.\n- The key curation rule in the reference code is ROI filtering by suite2p `iscell`. If `track_ops.iscell_thr` is `None`, keep rows with `iscell[:, 0] == 1`; otherwise keep rows with `iscell[:, 1] > iscell_thr`. The default threshold is `0.50`.\n- The code preserves suite2p trace arrays directly (`F.npy`, `Fneu.npy`, `spks.npy`) after `iscell` filtering and cell matching. It does not compute `dF/F` anywhere in the repository sections inspected for loading/export, so conversion should not invent a `dF/F` transform unless raw data forces it.\n- `save_in_s2p_format()` first removes cells not matched across all days in a match matrix row (`t2p_match_mat_allday = t2p_match_mat[~np.any(t2p_match_mat == None, axis=1), :]`) and then indexes filtered `F/Fneu/spks/stat/iscell` arrays with those matched-cell indices. This is the core longitudinal-neuron selection logic.\n- Mean images and ROI shapes are used for registration/visualization only. For downstream decoding, the relevant reference outputs are the matched neural traces and the suite2p metadata (`ops`, `stat`, `iscell`, match matrices, suite2p indices).\n- The code assumes imaging data in suite2p organization, supports multiple planes, and can save one matched suite2p folder per session/day under `track2p/matched_suite2p/`.\n- No code related to motion-energy extraction, trialization, temporal binning for decoder inputs/outputs, or session splitting into 60 s trials appears in the reference repository. Those parts will need to come from the raw data organization and paper/methods, while respecting the above suite2p/Track2p cell filtering and matching decisions.\n\n---\n\n## Step 2: Dataset Exploration\n**Status**: COMPLETE\n\n### Data Structure\n- `/app/data/README.md` describes the release and folder organization.\n- There are 6 subject folders: `jm031`, `jm032`, `jm038`, `jm039`, `jm040`, `jm046`.\n- Each subject contains daily session folders named by recording date plus `_a` suffix.\n- Each session contains:\n  - `suite2p/plane0/`\n    - `F.npy`: raw fluorescence traces, shape `(n_neurons, n_frames)`, `float32`\n    - `Fneu.npy`: neuropil fluorescence traces\n    - `spks.npy`: suite2p deconvolved activity, shape `(n_neurons, n_frames)`, `float32`\n    - `iscell.npy`: shape `(n_neurons, 2)`; already matched/exported Track2p cells\n    - `ops.npy`: suite2p metadata, includes `fs=30`, `nframes`, `meanImg`\n    - `stat.npy`: suite2p ROI metadata per cell\n  - `move_deve/`\n    - `motion_energy_glob.npy`: processed global motion energy from behavior video, one value per available camera frame\n    - `tstamps.npy`: timestamps for available behavior frames\n    - `interframe_int.npy`: inter-frame intervals used to identify dropped/missing camera frames\n- `load_data.ipynb` in `/app/data` provides example loading. It explicitly says `F.npy` contains raw fluorescence and suggests either computing `dF/F` “the way as described in the paper” or using `spks.npy` directly.\n- `ground_truth.csv` exists for `jm038`, `jm039`, and `jm046`. It is a semicolon-separated table of manual cross-day cell correspondences (`D1`-`D7` columns). This looks useful for Track2p evaluation, not for the requested decoder.\n- All inspected sessions have exactly one suite2p plane: `plane0`.\n- The suite2p folders already contain neurons “present across all days” for that subject, so neuron row identities are aligned across days within each subject per `/app/data/README.md`.\n\n### Dataset Size (from data files)\n| Statistic | Value |\n|-----------|-------|\n| Neurons (total) | 2,998 unique matched neurons across subjects; 20,445 neuron-session entries summed across all sessions |\n| Neurons / session | Subject-specific constant counts: 221 (`jm031`), 370 (`jm032`), 685 (`jm038`), 746 (`jm039`), 541 (`jm040`), 435 (`jm046`) |\n| Subjects | 6 |\n| Sessions / subject | `jm031`: 7, `jm032`: 7, `jm038`: 7, `jm039`: 7, `jm040`: 6, `jm046`: 7 |\n| Trials (total) | No native trials; continuous recordings. If split into 60 s windows at 30 Hz, there are 1,090 exact full windows total |\n| Trials / session | No native trials; 20 windows for 36,000-frame sessions and 30 windows for 54,000-frame sessions |\n\nAdditional native-data observations:\n- 41 sessions total.\n- Session frame counts are either 36,000 frames (20 min at 30 Hz) or 54,000 frames (30 min at 30 Hz).\n- `ops['nframes']` matches `F.shape[1]` in inspected sessions.\n- `motion_energy_glob.npy` and `tstamps.npy` always have the same length.\n- In 32/41 sessions, motion length matches imaging frame count exactly.\n- In 9/41 sessions, camera-frame samples are missing relative to imaging frames by 1, 2, 3, 116, or 148 frames depending on session.\n- Missing behavior frames are visible as approximately doubled intervals in `interframe_int.npy`, consistent with `/app/data/README.md`.\n- `tstamps.npy` appears to be in kiloseconds rather than seconds: `(session_duration_seconds) / (tstamps[-1] - tstamps[0])` is consistently about 992, so multiplying timestamps by 1000 should recover near-second units.\n\n---\n\n## Step 3: Reference Text Reading\n**Status**: COMPLETE\n\n### Expected Statistics (from paper/methods)\n| Statistic | Value | Source Quote |\n|-----------|-------|--------------|\n| Neurons (total) | Average 526 ± 190 tracked neurons per mouse across all days | “On average 526 (± 190 std) neurons per mouse were successfully tracked across all days using Track2p” |\n| Neurons / session | Hundreds of tracked neurons per mouse/day; same tracked population across days within mouse | “yielded hundreds of identified neurons tracked across all days” |\n| Subjects | 6 mice | “we used a full dataset of 6 mice imaged daily” |\n| Sessions / subject | Minimum 6 consecutive daily sessions; example dataset 7 sessions from one mouse | “for at least 6 consecutive days” and “n=7 imaging sessions from one mouse” |\n| Trials (total) | No native trials described; recordings are continuous sessions | Paper describes continuous recordings and 2 min blocks for cross-validation, not trialized data |\n| Trials / session | Not applicable natively; session is continuous | “each session lasted 20 minutes” |\n| Neural data time bin | Native sampling 30 Hz; decoding analyses average 10 timestamps | “Imaging rate was 30 Hz” and “averaging in bins of 10 consecutive timestamps” |\n| Behavior data time bin | Native videography 30 Hz; decoding analyses average 10 timestamps | “Videos were recorded at 30 Hz” and “averaging in bins of 10 consecutive timestamps” |\n| Reward rate | Not applicable | No reward-based task; spontaneous behavior dataset |\n| Motion variable | Global motion energy from squared pixelwise frame differences | “computed their pixelwise difference… squared all individual pixel-wise values and summed across pixels” |\n| Decoder evaluation metric | Continuous regression performance reported as R2, same-day and cross-day | “Graphs indicating R2 values for same-day cross-validated decoding performance” |\n| Developmental transition | Behavioral-state representation emerges around P11 | “marking the emergence of a stable behavioral state representation” |\n\n\n### Processing Details\n- Imaging modality: chronic 2-photon calcium imaging in mouse barrel cortex layer 2/3, 512×512 pixels, 720×720 µm field of view.\n- Sampling/alignment: imaging at 30 Hz; videography at 30 Hz with microscope acquisition triggering camera frames, described as allowing “simple synchronisation across the two modalities.”\n- Neural preprocessing used in paper: Suite2p motion correction, ROI detection, signal extraction, spike deconvolution, then keep ROIs with Suite2p cell-classifier probability above 0.5.\n- Neural signal used for downstream analyses in paper: baseline-corrected fluorescence traces treated as dF/F using default Suite2p parameters.\n- Behavioral preprocessing: motion energy computed from consecutive video-frame differences, squaring pixel differences and summing over pixels to get one scalar per timepoint.\n- Decoder preprocessing in paper: both dF/F and behavior traces are slightly denoised by averaging in bins of 10 consecutive timestamps (at 30 Hz this corresponds to ~333 ms bins).\n- Same-day decoder evaluation in paper: nested cross-validation with inner and outer 5-fold loops, splitting recordings into consecutive 2-minute blocks.\n- Cross-day decoder evaluation in paper: fit on one day, test on all other days using the tracked matched-neuron population.\n\n### Curation Steps\n\n**Neuron curation rules**:\n- Use Suite2p preprocessing separately on each recording.\n- Accept ROIs classified as true cells using the default Suite2p threshold of 0.5.\n- Track cells across days with Track2p and analyze the neurons successfully tracked across all recorded days for a mouse.\n\n**Trial curation rules**:\n- No native trials are described in the paper; recordings are continuous spontaneous-behavior sessions.\n- Decoder evaluation blocks in the paper use consecutive 2-minute chunks for cross-validation, but the user task explicitly requires conversion into 60-second trials for downstream decoding.\n\n### Decoders Trained\n| Decoded variable | Accuracy |\n| Mouse motion / behavioral state from neural population activity | No explicit numeric table in text; figure captions/reporting use R2 and state that same-day decoding increases with development and late cross-day decoding is accurate/stable |\n\n---\n\n## Step 4: Check for Consistency\n**Status**: COMPLETE\n\n### Discrepancies Found\n| Topic | Code says | Data shows | Paper says | Resolution |\n|-------|-----------|------------|------------|------------|\n| Neural data representation | Track2p code exports matched `F`, `Fneu`, `spks`, `stat`, `iscell` in suite2p format; no `dF/F` computation inside repository | Release contains matched `F.npy`, `Fneu.npy`, `spks.npy`, `ops.npy`, `stat.npy`, `iscell.npy` per session | Downstream analyses used “baseline corrected fluorescence traces as our dF/F (using the default Suite2p parameters)” | Treat Track2p output as the matched-neuron substrate and reproduce Suite2p-style dF/F preprocessing from the saved suite2p arrays/ops when building decoder inputs. If exact reconstruction proves impossible, document the fallback. |\n| Cell filtering | Reference code applies `iscell[:,1] > 0.5` by default | Released matched sessions already satisfy this: minimum observed `iscell[:,1]` across sessions is `0.500251773...` | Paper says “We considered all ROIs above the default threshold of 0.5 as true cells.” | No extra ROI filtering beyond the released matched suite2p files is needed; the data are already filtered consistently with code/paper. |\n| Longitudinal matching | Track2p `save_in_s2p_format()` keeps only rows matched across all days for a mouse | Data README states neurons are “present across all days” and row identities match across days within mouse; neuron count is constant within a subject across sessions | Paper reports neurons “tracked across all days” for downstream analyses | Use the provided session files directly as already matched across all recorded days per subject; no need to rerun Track2p. |\n| Session duration | Track2p code is agnostic to duration | Sessions are either 36,000 frames (20 min) or 54,000 frames (30 min) at 30 Hz | Methods text says “each session lasted 20 minutes” | Use actual frame counts in the released data as the source of truth and document the mismatch. This likely reflects heterogeneity between cohorts or an imprecise summary in the manuscript. |\n| Behavior-neural alignment | Track2p code does not handle behavior | Motion arrays usually match imaging length, but 9 sessions have missing behavior frames detectable in `tstamps.npy`/`interframe_int.npy` | Data README explicitly mentions missing camera frames and says missing-frame indices can be obtained from timestamps / interframe intervals; methods say camera was triggered by microscope at 30 Hz | Reconstruct per-imaging-frame behavior by using timestamps/interframe intervals to place available motion samples on the full imaging frame grid, then interpolate or otherwise fill only the missing camera-frame positions. |\n| Decoder protocol | Track2p repository has no decoder | Data are continuous recordings, not trials | Paper decoding uses ridge regression on continuous traces, 10-frame averaging, and 2-minute CV blocks | For the requested deliverable, preserve the paper’s 10-frame denoising/binning but adapt the representation to the user’s 60-second trial format and categorical output requirement. |\n\n---\n\n## Step 5: Mapping Planning\n**Status**: COMPLETE\n\n### Variable Mapping\n| Source Variable | Target Field | Transform | Reference Code Function(s) | Notes |\n|-----------------|--------------|-----------|----------------------------|-------|\n| `suite2p/plane0/F.npy` + `suite2p/plane0/Fneu.npy` + `suite2p/plane0/ops.npy` | `neural` | Compute `dF = F - ops['neucoeff'] * Fneu`, then apply Suite2p `dcnv.preprocess()` with saved default params (`baseline`, `win_baseline`, `sig_baseline`, `fs`, `prctile_baseline`, `batch_size`); average in non-overlapping 10-frame bins; split into 60 s trials; transpose to `(n_neurons, n_timepoints)` per trial | Track2p `save_in_s2p_format()` preserves matched `F/Fneu`; Suite2p `pipeline_s2p.py` deconvolution path shows `dF = F - neucoeff*Fneu` then `preprocess()` | Chosen to match the paper’s downstream analyses, which use Suite2p baseline-corrected fluorescence traces as dF/F. |\n| Imaging frame index / `ops['fs']` | `input[0]` | Construct elapsed-time array in seconds on the imaging axis, then average in the same 10-frame bins and split into 60 s trials. Use absolute time from session start, not time-within-trial | Not from Track2p code; required by user decoder spec | One input dimension named `time_from_session_start_sec`. |\n| `move_deve/motion_energy_glob.npy` + `tstamps.npy` + `interframe_int.npy` | `output[0]` | Reconstruct full per-imaging-frame motion series by assigning measured motion to frame indices inferred from timestamps and filling missing camera frames by interpolation; average in non-overlapping 10-frame bins; compute session-specific quintile edges from the full binned session; discretize into 5 categories; split into 60 s trials | No corresponding Track2p code; follows data README + paper motion-energy definition | One time-varying categorical output named `motion_energy_bin`; categories are session-specific quintiles. |\n| Subject folder name (e.g. `jm031`) | `subjects`, `subject_idx` | Unique sorted subject list; per-session subject index | N/A | Session order will follow subject/date sorting for reproducibility. |\n| Paper/methods metadata | `brain_regions`, `brain_region_idx` | Single region list `['barrel cortex L2/3']`; all neurons map to region index 0 | N/A | All recordings are from barrel cortex layer 2/3. |\n\n### Key Decisions\n1. **Use matched Suite2p exports directly**: The release already contains Track2p outputs restricted to neurons present across all days within each mouse, which matches the paper’s downstream analysis population and avoids rerunning the tracking algorithm.\n2. **Use Suite2p-style baseline-corrected fluorescence rather than raw `F.npy` or `spks.npy` for `neural`**: The paper explicitly states that subsequent analyses, including decoding, used baseline-corrected fluorescence traces as dF/F. Because `F.npy` is raw fluorescence and `Fneu.npy` plus `ops.npy` are available, reproducing the Suite2p preprocessing is better matched to the reference than using `spks.npy`.\n3. **Apply the paper’s 10-frame averaging before decoder formatting**: This preserves the denoising/binning used in the reference decoding while reducing dimensionality. At 30 Hz, 10 frames = 1/3 s bins, so 60 s trials contain exactly 180 time bins.\n4. **Use imaging frames as the canonical time axis**: Neural traces are complete on this axis; behavior is mapped onto it. This avoids drifting to the shorter camera series in sessions with dropped video frames.\n5. **Interpolate only missing camera-frame positions**: The data README explicitly points to timestamps/inter-frame intervals for handling missing camera frames. Filling only inferred missing samples preserves measured motion everywhere else while producing aligned dense behavior for the decoder.\n6. **Split sessions into contiguous 60 s windows without overlap**: This matches the user requirement and is exact because all sessions are multiples of 1,800 imaging frames (60 s at 30 Hz).\n7. **Use session-specific motion-energy quintiles after denoising**: The user requires five equal-percentile bins “selected per session.” Applying percentiles on the binned behavior series aligns the labels with the actual decoder target time base.\n8. **Keep all sessions and all full windows**: Every session yields at least 20 trials, so no session needs exclusion for insufficient trials. No extra neuron/session filtering beyond the provided matched-cell release is justified.\n9. **Represent input as absolute session time in seconds**: The user asked specifically for time elapsed from session start. This variable should continue increasing across trials rather than resetting at each 60 s boundary.\n\n### Planned Sanity Checks\n- [ ] Neural preprocessing sanity check: for selected neurons/session, verify converted neural bins equal manual computation from raw `F`, `Fneu`, `ops`, Suite2p `preprocess()`, and 10-frame averaging via `np.allclose()`.\n- [ ] Behavior alignment sanity check: for a no-drop session, verify reconstructed full motion equals raw `motion_energy_glob.npy` exactly; for a drop session, verify inferred missing-frame count equals `n_imaging_frames - len(motion)` and non-missing positions match exactly.\n- [ ] Trialization sanity check: verify each trial is exactly 180 bins and concatenating all trials reconstructs the full-session binned arrays.\n- [ ] Output discretization sanity check: verify per-session class fractions are approximately 0.2 each after quintile binning, allowing small deviations from ties.\n- [ ] Metadata/count sanity check: verify subject count, session count, neurons/session, and trials/session match the raw data and paper-derived expectations.\n\n---\n\n## Step 6: Script Development\n**Status**: COMPLETE\n\nImplemented `/app/convert_data.py` with the following structure:\n- Session discovery from `/app/data` with deterministic ordering.\n- Representative `--sample` mode selecting two sessions that exercise both a dropped-camera-frames edge case and a different session duration.\n- Suite2p-style neural preprocessing:\n  - load `F.npy`, `Fneu.npy`, `ops.npy`\n  - compute neuropil-subtracted traces `F - neucoeff * Fneu`\n  - apply `suite2p.extraction.dcnv.preprocess()` using saved per-session Suite2p parameters\n- Behavior alignment:\n  - infer available behavior-frame positions from `tstamps.npy`\n  - reconstruct a dense motion-energy series on the imaging frame grid\n  - linearly interpolate only missing camera-frame positions\n- Downstream formatting:\n  - average neural, time, and motion traces in 10-frame bins\n  - discretize binned motion into five session-specific bins\n  - split each session into non-overlapping 60 s trials\n  - package as the required dictionary and save to pickle\n- Processing visualizations saved as `processing_<subject>_<session>.png` when `--show-processing` is used.\n- Runtime reporting with per-session timing and ETA.\n\nCode inefficiencies identified:\n- Plot generation reloads `ops.npy` once inside the plotting helper; negligible for current scale but avoidable if necessary.\n- Conversion is currently single-process by session; likely acceptable for 41 sessions, but full-run timing will determine whether further optimization is required.\n\nCode speedups added:\n- Use `mmap_mode='r'` when discovering sessions so cataloging does not load full arrays.\n- Process one session at a time to cap peak memory.\n- Use vectorized reshape/mean operations for 10-frame binning.\n- Avoid temporary concatenations when trializing by slicing already binned arrays.\n- First execution test via `python -u /app/convert_data.py /app/sample_data.pkl --sample --show-processing` completed without errors.\n\n---\n\n## Step 7: Sample Conversion and Split sessions into 60-second trials.\\n\\n### Decoder Inputs:\\n- Time elapsed from the beginning of the session in seconds. Time-varying.\\n\\n### Decoder Outputs\\n- Motion energy, discretized into five equal-percentile bins, selected per session. Time-varying.\\n\\n## Target Data Format\\n\\n- Save the full converted dataset to the pickle file `/app/converted_data.pkl`.\\n- All datasets must be converted into the following Python dictionary structure:\\n\\n```python\\ndata = {\\n    'neural': [  # list of sessions\\n        [  # list of trials for session 1\\n            neuron_by_time_matrix,  # shape: (n_neurons, n_timepoints)\\n            neuron_by_time_matrix,  # trial 2\\n            ...\\n        ],\\n        [  # list of trials for session 2\\n            ...\\n        ],\\n        ...\\n    ],\\n\\n    'input': [  # list of sessions\\n        [  # list of trials for session 1\\n            input_data,  # stimulus/task variables, shape: (n_input, n_timepoints) or (n_input)\\n            ...\\n        ],\\n        ...\\n    ],\\n\\n    'output': [  # list of sessions\\n        [  # list of trials for session 1\\n            output_data,  # behavioral response, shape (n_output, n_timepoints) or (n_output)\\n            ...\\n        ],\\n        ...\\n    ],\\n\\n    'subjects': list of str, # ids of all subjects, length n_subjects\\n    'subject_idx': shape (n_sessions,), # index into subjects for each session\\n\\n    'brain_regions': list of str, # names of the brain regions recorded from, e.g. ALM, V1\\n    'brain_region_idx': [ # list of sessions\\n        neuron_region_idx, # shape: (n_neurons,)\\n    ], # index into `brain_regions` for each neuron\\n\\n    'input_names': list of str, # names for each input, of length n_input\\n    'output_names': list of str, # They could be task inputs to the animal, but inputs to the animal can also be decoder outputs.\\n  - Examples: time from an aligning signal, stimulus characteristics, our animal behavior such as:\\n    - visual: contrast, position, orientation\\n    - audio: frequency, amplitude\\n    - behavior: running speed, pupil size\\n  - Dimensions: (d_input, n_timepoints) for each trial. \\n  - Should capture all relevant contextual information for the decoder\\n  - If an input is a time such as onset of some stimulus, represent it as a binary time series. \\n\\n- **output**: Variables to be decoded/predicted from neural activity\\n  - These are what we want the decoder to **predict**. They could be the animal's output behavior or properties of the stimulus.\\n  - Output variables must be **categorical**, i.e. discrete rather than continuous. If a variable is not discrete, follow instructions from Decoder Task to discretize it. \\n  - Examples: stimulus properties (contrast, orientation), choice (left/right), reaction time, position\\n  - Can be time-varying or discrete values per trial. Negative means before event, positive after. \\n  - `off_end`: Signed time from alignment event to end of trial\\n  - Add other relevant fields, e.g. `session_info`\\n\\n## Python environment\\n\\n- All required Python packages are already installed in the system Python environment.\\n- Use `python3` to run any python code.\\n- If you need to install additional libraries, use `pip install`.\\n\\n## Decoder Reference\\n\\n- Run this script with `python /app/train_decoder.py <data_file_path>` to validate.\\n- Optional arguments:\\n  - `--verify-only`: Only verify data format and print summary, then exit (does not train decoder).\\n  - `--plot-samples`: Plot sample trials from data and save to png files.\\n  - `--cpu`: Force torch to use CPU only (use if GPU runs out of memory).\\n- Pipe the output to the designated file so that the user can examine it.\\n\\n### Purpose\\n\\nThese functions will be used during the validation phase to ensure:\\n1.
